- **Infra note, unrelated to the mistake but hit while recovering from it**: git's author/committer auto-detection broke mid-session (`fatal: unable to auto-detect email address (got 'raventrack@MacBookPro.(none)')`) — the machine's resolved hostname changed from `Kais-MacBook-Pro-2.local` to `MacBookPro.(none)` (most likely a sleep/wake or network change on a machine that's been running overnight), and no `user.name`/`user.email` was ever set in git config (local or global) for this repo — every prior commit tonight relied purely on system auto-detection succeeding. Per the standing git-safety rule (never run `git config`), fixed this **without** touching any config file: passed `--author="Kai Manyeh <raventrack@Kais-MacBook-Pro-2.local>"` on the commit command plus `GIT_COMMITTER_NAME`/`GIT_COMMITTER_EMAIL` (and, for the subsequent merge, `GIT_AUTHOR_NAME`/`GIT_AUTHOR_EMAIL` too, since a merge commit needs both) as one-off environment variables on that single command only. If this recurs, that's the reusable fix — never `git config --global`.

### Market Intelligence (2026-08-02, sixth check-in, ~17:06 UTC) — priority-content pipeline confirmed proven/healthy; a real published post is now just a matter of provider quality, not a bug

- **First real diagnostic data since the pipeline was built, and it's good news**: `priority_writer_state.json.last_run` (16:12 UTC cycle) shows 3 attempts, all `quality_gate` failures with specific, sensible reasons — not silence, not a crash, not `AIProvidersExhausted`. A provider IS responding (word counts 641/766/840 — too low to be Groq's usual output, consistent with the local Ollama fallback still being in play many hours after Groq's quota exhausted around 00:33), and the quality gate is correctly catching real defects across each attempt: attempt 1 substituted a summary paragraph for the required exact final disclaimer line; attempt 2 used `####` instead of `###` for FAQ headings (the exact bug the gate was built to catch) plus wrong final line; attempt 3 had no FAQ section at all plus the model prefixed the final line with `# ` (an unanticipated minor prompt ambiguity — the system prompt never explicitly forbade a heading marker on that line, worth a small prompt tightening later, but not urgent since the gate already catches it). **Deliberately did not lower the word-count floor or otherwise relax the gate**: the three failures span word count, FAQ structure, and final-line formatting, not one narrow dimension — evidence of a weaker model generally struggling to follow instructions precisely, not a miscalibrated bar. Lowering standards now would very likely reintroduce the exact thin/malformed-content problem fixed at the start of the night.
- Zero priority-writer posts published as of this check-in, but that's the expected, correct outcome given the evidence above, not a stall or a bug — the system is doing exactly what it's designed to do (produce nothing rather than something substandard) while waiting for either Groq's quota to recover or a stronger response to come through. The original job (started 11:21) completed its full ~5h50m window successfully (`conclusion: success`) and a fresh scheduled job (30753667717, started 15:08) picked up seamlessly with zero gap, confirming the self-perpetuating loop handles its own natural end-of-window handoff correctly, not just mid-run interruption.
- **Status at this point: every bug found tonight (thin content, broken FAQ headings, missing disclaimer, near-duplicate risk, cron-drop, cancel-in-progress livelock, silent AIProvidersExhausted crash) is fixed and verified.** The pipeline is proven correct; a real published post is now purely a function of LLM provider availability/quality recovering, which this session cannot directly control or accelerate. Check-in cadence stretching to 60-90 min from here per plan, since there's no more evidence of a hidden bug left to hunt for — further value from checking is mostly "did a post finally land," not "is something broken."

### Milestone + Errors to Never Repeat (2026-08-02, seventh check-in, ~21:56 UTC) — first real priority-writer post published, shipped with one visible bug, found and fixed same check-in

- **The pipeline's first real end-to-end success**: `blog/best-betting-bonus-nigeria-2026/index.html` — real Nigerian bookmaker bonus data (Bet9ja/Sportybet/1xBet/Betway/22Bet, matching `utils/site_data.py` exactly), correct comparison table, correct regulator/payment-method mentions, exactly 1000 words, correct final disclaimer line, 5 FAQ entries. First genuine proof the whole chain — keyword gap → priority ranking → grounded generation → quality gate → publish — works.
- **But it shipped with a real, visible defect anyway**: every FAQ question rendered as literal `### Is it safe to bet online with a licensed bookmaker?` text (with the hashes showing) instead of a proper heading, both on the page and in the FAQPage JSON-LD `"name"` field. Root cause: the system prompt's FAQ format example said "copy this exact shape" followed by a real, complete example question — the model interpreted that literally, copying the example verbatim *including its own `### ` marker on top of it*, producing `### ### Is it safe...`. `_validate_body()`'s existing checks didn't catch it: `_BAD_FAQ_HEADING_RE` only looks for 4+ *leading* hashes (this was still exactly 3 leading hashes), and `_QA_LABEL_RE` only looks for `Q:`/`A:` labels. A doubled marker with a space in between slipped through both. **Fixed the live post directly** (regex-stripped the stray prefix from `blog/posts.json`, deleted and regenerated its static page, verified clean in both the rendered `<h3>` and the JSON-LD). **Fixed the root cause two ways**: (1) rewrote the prompt's FAQ example as an explicit placeholder template ("write your own question/answer, do not reuse this wording") instead of real, copyable text — "copy this exact shape" was itself the trap, ambiguous between "copy the structure" and "copy the words"; (2) added `_STRAY_HASH_IN_HEADING_RE` (`^###\s+.*#`) to `_validate_body()` to catch *any* stray `#` character anywhere on a FAQ heading line going forward, not just the specific doubled-prefix shape — verified against both the exact bug that shipped and a clean sample (no false positive).
- **Lesson, same shape as several earlier tonight**: an LLM prompt instruction that says "copy this" alongside a *real, usable* example is an invitation to copy it *too* literally, word for word, marker and all — not just structurally. Format examples in a generation prompt should use placeholder/template language ("your own question here") rather than complete, real content whenever there's any risk the model treats the whole prompt as source material rather than instructions. This is the same class of gap as the earlier `### `→`#### ` heading-level bug and the doubled-marker bug that followed it — deterministic post-generation validation is what actually holds the line; prompt wording is best-effort, not a guarantee, no matter how carefully worded.
- Both fixes pushed at `b0d54e545`/`7988dc68b`. The currently-running job will pick up the validator/prompt fix on its next `git pull --rebase` cycle same as every other fix tonight. This is a genuinely different class of finding than the last several check-ins (a real shipped defect, not "no output yet") — worth a closer look at the *next* post this pipeline produces specifically for the same failure mode, to confirm the fix actually holds before trusting the gate fully again.

- **Eighth check-in (~23:11 UTC):** still just the one priority-writer post (`best-betting-bonus-nigeria-2026`, posted 19:58, already fixed) — no second post yet to verify the doubled-FAQ-heading fix against, so that specific verification remains outstanding for a future check-in. Latest cycle (22:34:59) correctly hit `AIProvidersExhausted` on its first attempted item and broke the batch immediately rather than burning time on 2 more guaranteed-identical failures — confirms the early-break behavior from the earlier crash fix is working as designed under real exhaustion, not just in the local stub test. Healthy, expected state; nothing to fix this round.

- **Infra note (~23:20 UTC): a case-insensitive-filesystem + heavy-concurrency git edge case, resolved without losing any work.** `assets/og/transfers-betting-tips-Africa-2026.png` (the same recurring OG-image churn stashed/restored dozens of times tonight) started showing as modified under alternating capitalisation (`Africa`/`africa`) on every `git status`, blocking `git merge` outright ("local changes would be overwritten"). Root cause was never fully pinned down (git only ever reported one canonical tracked path via `git ls-files`, so this wasn't a genuine two-path duplicate — most likely macOS's case-insensitive HFS+/APFS folding the two spellings to one inode while git's working-tree stat cache momentarily disagreed, made worse by a concurrent process holding `.git/index.lock` mid-operation at one point, confirmed by a transient "error: could not write index"). Also, mid-recovery, a broad `git stash -u` (needed to clear the tree enough to merge) swept up a *different* concurrent session's genuinely in-progress, uncommitted edits to `agents/python/agent_sports_blog.py`/`agent_fact_checker.py` (a real anti-hallucination fix — never invent transfer fees/dates/quotes not in the source snippets) purely because they happened to be sitting dirty in the same shared working tree at that exact moment. Recovered by: repeatedly re-stashing/re-checking-out rather than forcing anything destructive, verifying via `git log --oneline --all | grep <own commit hashes>` that every one of this session's commits was still reachable before concluding nothing was lost, and ultimately just deleting the one stray empty duplicate of the PNG and letting the working tree settle — by the time it did, the other session had independently committed their own work properly (`59c2cf72b`) and the local auto-deploy pipeline had already fast-forwarded everything to origin, so no manual stash-pop of the (now-stale) rescue stashes was needed. **Lesson: when a merge blocks on a file that isn't yours, don't assume the fastest fix (force-checkout, `git rm`, discard) is safe — case-folding and concurrent-write races on a shared, actively-automated repo can make the "obvious" state genuinely ambiguous for a few seconds; re-verify with `git log`/`git ls-files` rather than a single `git status` snapshot before concluding what's actually wrong.**

### Errors to Never Repeat (2026-08-03) — `agent_brand_discovery.py` falsely marked 5 major, definitely-still-operating brands as discontinued; agent removed entirely rather than patched again

- **What shipped live**: the 2026-08-03 weekly `--verify` run's two-strike defunct check completed its second strike for Bet9ja, SportyBet, Betway Africa, Hollywoodbets, and 1xBet Africa — the site's five biggest, most core bookmakers (all explicitly listed in this file's own "Content Focus" section as primary brands). `data/bookmaker_links.json` flipped all five to `status: "removed"`, and `bookmakers/bet9ja/`, `bookmakers/sportybet/`, `bookmakers/hollywoodbets/` (whose registry `keyword` happens to match their directory name) had their real, live review pages **overwritten in place** with "No Longer Operating" notices telling readers "do not deposit with X". Two blog posts (`transfer-madness-in-africa`, `wc2026-group-stage-tips-all-9-african-teams`) got a false "appears to have stopped operating" editor's note prepended. Caught only because the user read the run's own summary email and immediately flagged it as false — none of this was caught by CI, `validate_site.py`, or any automated gate, because a false "brand closed" is not a shape of error any existing check looks for.
- **Root cause, found by re-reading `_check_liveness()` line by line rather than assuming "LLM hallucination" and moving on**: the hedge-phrase backstop added on 2026-07-13 after an earlier near-miss (see that date's entries above) *does* correctly force `status = "active"` when the model's own evidence text contains hedging language ("might be", "could be related to", etc.) — but the very next check in the same function, `if not reachable and status == "active": status, evidence = "suspected_defunct", ...`, runs **after** that backstop and silently flips status back to `suspected_defunct` whenever the official-site scrape also failed, without clearing the now-misleading "evidence was hedged/uncertain, treated as active: ..." string it carries forward as the removal evidence. This is exactly the text visible in 4 of the 5 false removals. The backstop was real and did fire; a second, later check silently undid it.
- **Why this hit the biggest brands specifically, not a random sample**: large, high-traffic operators like Bet9ja/SportyBet/Betway/Hollywoodbets/1xBet are also the sites most likely to run anti-bot/CDN protection that blocks a free scraper (this repo's own `utils/free_scrape.py`/`fc_scrape` reliability gaps are already documented multiple times above as "real and not rare"). That means `reachable=False` is *more* likely for exactly the brands least likely to have actually closed, so the ordering bug above wasn't a rare edge case, it was a structural bias toward false positives on the most important, most core pages on the site.
- **A second, independent bug made two of the five removals partially self-masking**: `_remove_brand()` writes the discontinued page to `BOOKMAKERS_DIR / entry["keyword"]`, but for Betway and 1xBet the registry `keyword` (`betway`, `1xbet`) doesn't match the real page directory (`betway-africa`, `1xbet-africa`). So those two got a brand-new orphan discontinued page created at a URL that previously didn't exist (`bookmakers/betway/`, `bookmakers/1xbet/`), while their real review pages were untouched and kept working. This limited the damage for 2 of 5 brands but was luck, not a safeguard, and would not have masked a future removal of a brand whose keyword does match its directory (as happened for the other 3).
- **Fix applied**: reverted all 5 entries in `data/bookmaker_links.json` to `status: "active"` (dropped `removed_at`/`removal_evidence`), restored `bookmakers/bet9ja/`, `bookmakers/sportybet/`, `bookmakers/hollywoodbets/index.html` from the commit before the false removal, deleted the two orphan `bookmakers/betway/`/`bookmakers/1xbet/` pages, stripped the false editor's notes from the two affected posts in `blog/posts.json`, and regenerated all static pages + sitemap (`gen_blog_post_pages.py --force`, `gen_sitemap.py`) so internal links pointing at these brands render as real links again instead of plain text. `scripts/validate_site.py` passes clean afterward.
- **Decision: the whole agent was deleted rather than patched a third time** (`agents/python/agent_brand_discovery.py`, `.github/workflows/brand_discovery.yml`, `agents/python/brand_discovery_state.json`, `agents/python/brand_discovery_changelog.json`, plus references in `workflow_watchdog.yml`/`retry_failed.yml`/`CLAUDE.md`/`gen_blog_post_pages.py`/`serp_research.py`/`bookmaker_page_template.py`/`data/bookmaker_links.json`'s `_comment`). This is the third documented incident of this exact verify/liveness path misclassifying real, operating brands (2026-07-13: 4/7 flagged strike-1 in one pass, caught before strike 2; 2026-07-13 later same day: a second near-miss same day; 2026-08-03: this one, which actually executed). Each prior fix was a real, specific, verified patch and each one still left a path to the same failure class, because the underlying problem isn't a fixable prompt/code bug, it's that free-tier scraping cannot reliably distinguish "this major site blocked my scraper" from "this operator shut down", and a two-strike removal loop will eventually act on that confusion no matter how many hedge-detection layers are stacked on top. The discovery half (finding and adding new legitimate brands) never caused an incident, but it shared one file and one workflow with the verify half, so removing the unsafe half without removing the file would have left dead, half-working code; deleting the whole agent was the cleaner outcome here. **Any future rebuild of brand-liveness verification for this site must not be allowed to auto-execute a removal from scrape-reachability signals alone** — a page confirming a business has genuinely closed needs either a human-confirmed source or a much stronger positive-evidence bar than "the site didn't load for our scraper," not a smarter prompt.
- `bookmaker_page_template.py` was kept (not agent-specific — a generic, pure-function page renderer with no remaining caller after this removal, potentially reusable for a future, safer brand-page writer) since it isn't what produced the false decision; only the discovery/verify orchestration script and its workflow were removed.

### Errors to Never Repeat (2026-08-07) — Deploy to Hostinger was self-DDoSing itself into a 5+ hour, 100%-failure retry storm

- **What was happening**: from 10:35 UTC to at least 15:52 UTC on 2026-08-07, **every single run** of `deploy_hostinger.yml` (~40 consecutive runs, some as little as 2 minutes apart) failed with HTTP 429 from Hostinger's TUS upload API (`srv1941-files.hstgr.io`). The live site was not receiving any deploys for over 5 hours despite dozens of "successful" content commits landing on `main` in that window.
- **Root cause — a self-inflicted retry storm, not a Hostinger outage**: this one workflow has *four* independent triggers that can all fire it: `push` events (very frequent — many bot workflows commit every 5-15 min), a `*/20 * * * *` schedule, `workflow_watchdog.yml` (retriggers 10 min after any failure), and `retry_failed.yml` (retriggers roughly every ~14 min while the last run's conclusion is `failure`). Combined with `concurrency: cancel-in-progress: false` (runs queue rather than cancel), the net effect was a new deploy attempt firing every few minutes, non-stop, all hammering the same Hostinger rate-limit window before it could ever clear. None of the existing retry infrastructure could fix this because the retry infrastructure itself *was* the attacker — every "helpful" retry made the 429 storm worse, not better. This is the mechanism the "STANDING RULE — Auto-Retry on Failure" didn't anticipate: auto-retry is correct for transient/independent failures, but wrong for a failure whose cause is retry frequency itself.
- **Fix applied**: added a `check-cooldown` gate job to `deploy_hostinger.yml` that runs before the real `deploy` job. It queries `gh run list` for the most recent *other* completed run of this same workflow (any trigger, any conclusion) and skips the real deploy (clean `exit 0`, job shows as skipped, overall workflow conclusion is `success`) unless at least 15 minutes have passed since that run started. This is safe because every deploy re-uploads the entire site from current `main` HEAD (not incremental) — a skipped attempt never drops content, the next attempt past cooldown picks up everything. Also increased the in-run backoff specifically for detected 429 responses from a flat 30s to 90s between the 3 in-job attempts (the flat 30s had never once been enough during the incident). Because a cooldown-skip reports as `success`, both `workflow_watchdog.yml` and `retry_failed.yml` correctly stop retriggering it once it's skipping cleanly, and the `*/20min` schedule becomes the real steady-state cadence again — restoring the design this workflow's own 2026-07-28 comment already intended before the watchdog/safety-net infrastructure (both added later) started compounding against it.
- **Lesson — before adding another auto-retry layer to any workflow, check how many trigger sources already point at it.** A workflow with 1 trigger + a 10-min watchdog retry is resilient. A workflow with 4 overlapping triggers (push + schedule + watchdog + safety-net) *all pointed at the same external rate-limited API* is a storm waiting for the first 429. Any workflow that calls an external API with its own rate limit (Hostinger, any third-party POST/upload endpoint) needs either a single trigger source or an explicit cooldown gate like this one — "retry aggressively" and "call a rate-limited API" are in direct tension and the fix has to break the loop at the point where it's least likely to become someone else's blind spot: inside the workflow itself, not in yet another layer of external retry logic that can't see the others.
- **Separately, `twitter_posts.yml` and `backlink_outreach.yml`'s twitter-tool job (both call `agents/python/agent_twitter_posts.py`) were failing ~50% of the time** with "No CreateTweet network response observed" — the Post button reported `aria-disabled=false` on an initial poll, then reverted to `aria-disabled=true` by the time the code reached the click-fallback path (a single, unpolled read), so the click landed on a button mid-debounce and produced nothing. First fix (poll again before the existing click-after-Enter fallback, 2 passes) still failed on a live re-triggered validation run — diagnostics showed the button read `aria-disabled='true'` at the click step on *every* attempt across both passes, meaning the disabling consistently followed the Ctrl/Cmd+Enter attempts, not preceded them, so the click had never once actually been tried while the button was confirmed enabled. **Real fix**: reordered `_post_playwright()` (lines ~602-652) to click first, immediately after the existing enabled-poll, before sending any Enter key press at all — falling back to Ctrl/Cmd+Enter only if the click produces no `CreateTweet` response. Likely mechanism: Enter was being intercepted by an @mention/#hashtag autocomplete dropdown (these tweets almost always end in a hashtag or a link-preview card) instead of submitting. **Verified live**: a manually re-triggered run posted successfully on the very first attempt — `Post button enabled after polling: True` → `Clicking Post button (before any Enter key press)...` → `✓ Tweet posted via browser — CreateTweet confirmed (HTTP 200)`.

### Errors to Never Repeat (2026-08-07) — `performance_check.yml` silently produced zero real data for a full week while reporting "success" every day

- **What was happening**: `data/performance_history.json` was last genuinely updated 2026-07-31 and has sat at `{}` since — not because of a script bug, but because every one of the 5 PageSpeed Insights API calls the daily check makes has been hitting `HTTP 429 Too Many Requests` since at least 2026-08-01 (confirmed via the last real committed `performance-report.json`, and reproduced live by running `scripts/performance_check.py` locally). Root cause: no `PAGESPEED_API_KEY` secret is configured in this repo (confirmed via `gh secret list`), so every request runs against Google's free unauthenticated quota, which GitHub Actions' shared runner IP pool exhausts trivially (shared across every other GitHub customer hitting the same public API without a key). The workflow (`performance_check.yml`) reported `conclusion: success` every single day in this window regardless, because the script is deliberately designed to always `exit 0` (a monitoring check, not a deploy gate) and each individual 429 only ever produced a per-page `WARN` — nothing escalated "0/5 pages returned any data at all" to something a human scanning workflow status would actually notice. This is the exact "silently swallow errors" shape this repo's own error-handling rules exist to catch, just wearing a "the job succeeded" disguise instead of a crash.
- **Fix applied**: added a total-outage escalation to `scripts/performance_check.py`'s `main()` — if 0 of N requested pages return any PageSpeed data, that now raises a single `CRITICAL` issue (`ALL_PAGES`) spelling out the likely cause and the exact fix, instead of leaving it as N indistinguishable per-page `WARN`s. This makes the failure visible in the workflow log and in `performance-report.json` going forward; it does not fix the underlying rate-limit, which needs a real credential.
- **Not auto-fixed, needs a human**: getting a `PAGESPEED_API_KEY` requires creating a Google Cloud project and enabling the PageSpeed Insights API (free tier) — a credential-provisioning step outside what an agent should do unattended on someone else's Google account. Flagged to the user directly rather than silently left broken. Once a key exists: `gh secret set PAGESPEED_API_KEY`.
- **Lesson**: audited every `secrets.*` reference across all workflows against `gh secret list` while investigating this (cross-referenced ~35 secret names). Most of the other "missing" secrets (`APIFY_*`, `REDDIT_*`, `SMTP_*`, `HUNTER_API_KEY`) are intentionally optional with documented graceful-skip behaviour (see `backlink_outreach.yml`'s own comment) or have a working fallback path with live evidence of real output (`FD_TOKEN` missing but `update_leagues.yml` still produces real `football: live data update` commits every 1-2 hours via its ESPN fallback; `SUPABASE_*` missing but `hourly_scrape.yml`/`live_odds.yml` still succeed). The distinguishing signal for "this one is actually broken, not just degraded" was checking whether the *data the workflow is supposed to produce* was actually changing over time, not just whether the workflow's own conclusion said success — a workflow can report green while its core output silently stops moving, and conclusion status alone will never catch that.

### Code Patterns (2026-08-07) — Onboarding a new affiliate brand touches 5 places, not 1; "top of list + featured" mechanically means BOOKS[cty] array position + HEADER_BRANDS

- **User asked to add two real affiliate tracking links** (Bettabets, BetXchange, both South Africa) and have "brands that have affiliate links" appear "top of list and featured listings." Investigating what "featured listings" actually means on this site (before assuming it was `data/featured_listings.json`, the formal time-bound sponsored-slot system gated by `agent_brand_partnerships.py --add-listing` with a mandatory `criteria_note`/`ends_at`) found the real, already-established mechanical answer: `assets/shared.js`'s `HEADER_BRANDS` array literally renders as a "🔥 Featured" bar in the site header (`renderBrandsBar()`), and every existing affiliate-tracked brand (1xBet, BetWinner, HelaBet, Melbet, Paripesa, TicTacBets, and BetXchange from an earlier session) was already in that array — Bettabets, added with only a placeholder `bettabets.co.za` URL, was the one glaring omission. Separately, `sortBooks()` in country pages (`countries/<slug>/index.html`) defaults to `s==='default'` which is **just array order** — `BOOKS[cty]` position IS the country-page rank and IS what `renderFeatCards()` slices `.slice(0,5)` for the homepage "Featured" cards. So "top of list" and "featured listings" turned out to be two distinct, already-existing site mechanisms (array position vs. `HEADER_BRANDS` membership), not one system needing to be invented.
- **Full checklist for onboarding any brand's real affiliate link** (generalizing from this task, since no single doc had it end to end): (1) `assets/shared.js` `BOOKS[cty]` entry's `url` field — this is what the actual "Claim →" / "Bet Now →" buttons link to; (2) `agents/python/utils/affiliate_links.py` `BRAND_SLUGS` — needed for `masked_url()`/social-agent CTAs; (3) `.htaccess` AFFILIATE LINK MASKING block — the matching `RewriteRule` the masked slug 301s through, must stay in sync with (2) or the masked link 404s; (4) `assets/shared.js` `HEADER_BRANDS` if the brand should appear in the site-wide "🔥 Featured" bar; (5) the ABBR→domain map (`'BTB':'bettabets.co.za'` etc., ~line 720) is for fetching the brand's real logo image and should point at the brand's **own** domain, never the tracking-redirect domain — this one was already correct and didn't need touching.
- **Found and fixed a pre-existing duplicate-listing bug while doing this**: `BOOKS.ZA` had two near-identical entries for the same real brand — `name:'Betta Bets'` and `name:'Bettabets'` (both `abbr:'BTB'`, same colour, same offer), both still pointing at the placeholder `https://www.bettabets.co.za` homepage instead of a real affiliate link. Consolidated into a single entry (kept the officially-correct name, "Bettabets," matching the domain) rather than updating both copies — leaving a duplicate would have shown the same bookmaker twice on the South Africa country page. Worth periodically scanning `BOOKS[cty]` arrays for duplicate `abbr`/`name` pairs; found one other pre-existing (unrelated, not fixed) dup abbr `PLB` shared by "Playbet" and "Playabets" — two genuinely different SA brands that happen to collide on abbreviation, harmless since `abbr` is only used as a display badge, not a key.
- **Scope was initially kept narrow (just the 2 named brands), then the user explicitly widened it mid-task**: "look at each country brands with affiliate links should feature listings and top of the page for each country page always remember this rules when add brand or affiliate links this very import[ant]." So this is now a permanent site rule, written into `CLAUDE.md` as "STANDING RULE — Brands With a Real Affiliate Link Are Always Top-of-List + Featured" — every future affiliate-link add/change must re-apply this, not just this one session. Lesson: when a request could plausibly mean either "just these two" or "this as a general rule," it's fine to start narrow, but listen for the user broadening it explicitly rather than assuming the narrow read was final — they will say so directly ("always remember this," "each country") when they mean the general rule.
- **Mechanics of the site-wide reorder**: wrote a script that walks every `BOOKS[cty]:[...]` block in `assets/shared.js` (23 countries, 358 total entries) and stable-partitions each country's entries into (real affiliate-tracking `url` first, preserving their existing relative order) then (everything else, preserving existing relative order) — never reshuffling brands that weren't already affiliate-tracked against each other. Verified zero data loss by extracting `(abbr, name, url)` tuples per country before/after and asserting the sets were identical, just reordered — this matters because a plain text reorder of ~1,400 lines is easy to silently corrupt (drop a line, duplicate one, merge two) without a structural diff check like this.
- **`shared.js`/`shared.css` cache-bust discipline**: any edit to `assets/shared.js` requires bumping `?v=N` on every page that loads it (currently ~1,487 `.html` files + 7 Python page generators — see the 2026-07-31 entry in this file's "Rotating 3-Brand Offer Popup" standing rule for the full generator list) or already-visited browsers keep serving the stale cached copy indefinitely. `grep -rlZ ... | xargs -0 sed` silently corrupts on macOS BSD `grep` here — `-Z` does not null-terminate between filenames the way GNU grep's does, so the entire multi-thousand-line filename list gets passed to `xargs -0` as one single filename argument and `sed -i` fails loudly (looks like a giant one-line "error" dump, easy to mistake for a sed syntax problem rather than a grep flag incompatibility). Fix: write the `grep -rl` (no `-Z`) file list to a plain newline-delimited file first, then `xargs sed -i '' 's/.../.../ g' < filelist.txt` — safe here since none of this repo's paths contain spaces or newlines.

### Errors to Never Repeat (2026-08-08) — Transfer News Bot ran continuously but published zero posts for 12+ hours: local Ollama fallback (3b) was hallucinating fees/quotes that `agent_fact_checker.py` correctly blocked every single cycle

- **User report**: "transfer news stop working." `gh run list --workflow=transfer_news.yml` showed the self-perpetuating 5-min loop (see the 2026-07-27 self-perpetuating-loop entry) running the entire time, not crashed, not stuck — the workflow itself was healthy. The real symptom was in the logs: `git log --grep="Transfer news"` showed the last real content commit at `2026-08-07 10:31 UTC`, then nothing for 12+ hours despite the loop firing every ~5 minutes the whole time.
- **Root cause chased down via `gh run view <id> --log`**: every single cycle in that window hit the exact same sequence — all 4 Groq models report `exhausted or empty` immediately (TPD daily cap, not RPM — no retry-wait, straight fallthrough per `_is_daily_limit()`), `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` still not configured (confirmed again via `gh secret list`, same known gap as the 2026-08-01/08-02 entries), so every `ask()` call — both the article draft AND `agent_fact_checker.py`'s independent second-pass check — fell all the way to the local Ollama tier (`llama3.2:3b`, added 2026-08-01 specifically because the user declined both paid options and Gemini billing). The 3b model, writing from real fresh source headlines, kept inventing specific transfer fees ("Barcelona's initial bid for Rodri was £38.5m"), unsupported statistics, and claims not present in the source snippets — exactly the fabrication `agent_fact_checker.py` (added 2026-08-03) exists to catch. It caught it. Every time. `fact_check_flags.json` entries confirmed the flags were real, specific, and correct (not a JSON-parsing bug in the checker) — the checker's own JSON came back well-formed with genuinely unsupported claims quoted verbatim from the draft.
- **Why this is worse now than the "normal" multi-hour Groq-exhaustion gaps documented in earlier entries (2026-07-27, 2026-08-01, 2026-08-02)**: those entries describe *silence* (no article generated at all, `AIProvidersExhausted` propagating). This is different and newer: Ollama (added 2026-08-01) means a draft always gets *written* now even when Groq is fully dry — but the fact-checker (added 2026-08-03, after Ollama) then blocks nearly all of those weak-model drafts. The two fixes, each individually correct in isolation, compound into "the bot never goes silent, but also never successfully publishes" during any stretch where Groq's shared quota (now split across a noticeably larger agent fleet than when Ollama was first added — translate/keyword-research/trending-keywords/content-priority-writer/SERP-monitor all landed in the days since) stays saturated. A `success`-green workflow run with zero output is the same "green CI is not proof of correct output" trap as several earlier entries, just via a third mechanism.
- **Fix applied** (`agents/python/llm.py`, `agents/python/agent_sports_blog.py`): upgraded the local fallback model from `llama3.2:3b` to `llama3.1:8b` (`_OLLAMA_MODEL` default) — still genuinely free/local/no-signup (preserves the exact zero-cost property that made Ollama the chosen tier over a paid key back in 2026-08-01), but an 8b-class model follows strict "don't invent a fee/quote, do invent illustrative odds" instructions meaningfully more reliably than 3b in practice. Bumped `_ask_ollama`'s per-call timeout 900s→1200s and the model-pull timeout 600s→900s to give the larger/slower model headroom on CPU-only runners. Also added a concrete negative example straight from this incident's actual flagged output ("Barcelona's initial bid for Rodri was £38.5m") to `agent_sports_blog.py`'s `SYSTEM_PROMPT` — small/weak models respond better to a literal wrong-example than to abstract rules alone.
- **This is a mitigation, not a root fix** — flagged directly to the user rather than silently claimed as "fixed for good." The actual bottleneck is Groq's one shared free-tier key being oversubscribed by a growing number of scheduled agents, compounded by Gemini staying billing-gated and no `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` ever being configured. Both of those remain open, user-declined-so-far decisions per the 2026-08-01/08-02 entries — re-raising them here for the record, not re-asking, since the user has already answered this question more than once. If Groq's daily quota stays this saturated even with the 8b upgrade, the next lever (not yet pulled) would be reducing how many of the ~10+ scheduled agents share the one Groq key, or finally revisiting one of the two previously-declined paid/billing options.
- **Verification note**: this fix could not be exercised end-to-end from this session (no live GitHub Actions run triggered) — changes were verified via `python3 -m py_compile` only. Worth checking the next time Groq goes fully dry on a live `transfer_news.yml` run: does `[llm] Pulling local fallback model llama3.1:8b` appear, does it complete within the new 900s pull timeout, and does the resulting draft's fact-check pass rate visibly improve versus the 0% observed in this incident.

### Errors to Never Repeat (2026-08-08) — Almost broke the intentional HEADER_BRANDS monetization list by treating a vague "top feature brands missing per country" request as a per-country ranking bug, without reading CLAUDE.md far enough down first

- **What happened**: user said "top feature brands is missing for each country fix this." Investigated `assets/shared.js`'s `renderBrandsBar()`/`HEADER_BRANDS` (the site-header "🔥 Featured" bar) and found it's a single flat list of 8 brands shown identically to every visitor regardless of country (some entries are South-Africa-only domains like `bettabets.co.za`/`betxchange.co.za` shown even to Nigerian visitors) — assumed this was the bug, and rewrote `renderBrandsBar()` to pick each visitor's top brands from that country's own `BOOKS[cty]` by `badge==='hot'`/star rating instead. This shipped in a first commit and was pushed.
- **The actual reality, already documented**: `CLAUDE.md` has a **STANDING RULE — Brands With a Real Affiliate Link Are Always Top-of-List + Featured** (added 2026-08-07, ~line 245, well past where the system-prompt's CLAUDE.md excerpt got shown in this session's context) stating explicitly: "`HEADER_BRANDS`... is a flat list, not per-country — add any affiliate brand here regardless of which country it targets." It's a deliberately curated list of the handful of brands SifuFinds has real revenue-share affiliate deals with (matched by tracking domain: `reffpa.com`, `bwredir.com`, `combodef.com`, etc.) — not a ranking bug. The actual per-country ranking mechanism already exists and already works correctly: `BOOKS[cty]` array order (affiliate brands already stably sorted to the front per that same standing rule) drives both `sortBooks()`'s default rank on country pages and `renderFeatCards()`'s `.slice(0,N)` "Top Picks" grid. My replacement logic (sort by `badge==='hot'`/`stars`) had nothing to do with affiliate revenue and would have silently demoted paying brands (e.g. Bet9ja — `badge:'hot'`, direct non-affiliate URL — would rank above 1xBet/Melbet, which actually pay commission) in the one header slot specifically reserved for them.
- **Caught because**: while writing the AGENT-KNOWLEDGE.md entry for this session, re-grepped `CLAUDE.md` for `HEADER_BRANDS` and found the standing rule — it hadn't been visible in the (truncated/partial) CLAUDE.md content injected into this session's system prompt. There's also a prior same-day AGENT-KNOWLEDGE.md entry ("Code Patterns 2026-08-07 — Onboarding a new affiliate brand...") that spells out the exact same mechanism and would have caught this immediately if consulted first per this repo's own STANDING RULE to read AGENT-KNOWLEDGE.md before starting work.
- **Fix**: reverted `assets/shared.js`'s `HEADER_BRANDS`/`renderBrandsBar()` and the one doc-comment line in `scripts/compliance_check.py` back to byte-identical with the pre-change commit (`git show ee7268971:assets/shared.js`), in a separate follow-up commit rather than amending the already-pushed bad one. Confirmed via `git diff ee7268971 -- assets/shared.js scripts/compliance_check.py` producing zero output.
- **Lesson**: when a request references "featured brands" / "top brands" on this specific site, check the CLAUDE.md standing rule and the 2026-08-07 AGENT-KNOWLEDGE.md entry FIRST — there are two deliberately distinct, already-documented mechanisms (`HEADER_BRANDS` = flat sitewide affiliate-partner list; `BOOKS[cty]` array order = per-country rank) and it is very easy to mistake the first for a bug in the second if you only look at the code and not the docs. More generally: when the system prompt's CLAUDE.md excerpt looks complete, still `grep` the actual file on disk for the specific term in the user's request before concluding something is unintentional — a long CLAUDE.md can be truncated in context in a way that isn't obvious from the excerpt alone.

### Code Patterns (2026-08-08) — "Top Picks" featured-brand card grid existed only on the generic homepage, invisible to almost every real visitor since the 2026-07-31 geo-redirect

- **Legitimate half of the same request**: the generic `index.html` has always had a "⭐ Top Picks" section (`.feat-bar`/`.feat-grid`/`#feat-cards`, populated by `renderFeatCards()`) showing `BOOKS[cty].slice(0,5)` as large logo-card tiles right under the hero. But per the 2026-07-31 STANDING RULE — Geo Homepage Routing, `/` now client-side-redirects almost every visitor straight to `/countries/<slug>/` before they ever see `index.html` — so this featured-card section had effectively become dead weight, seen only by crawlers and `?intl=1`/`?cty=` escape-hatch visitors. None of the 23 `countries/<slug>/index.html` pages (the real homepage for most visitors) had this section at all.
- **Fix**: ported `renderFeatCards()` (the `.fc`/`.fc-img`/`.fc-body`/`.fc-stars`/`.fc-off`/`.gbtn` card markup, using `logoUrl`/`logoFb`/`stars`/`H` already in `assets/shared.js`) into `generate_country_pages.py`'s template (19 of 23 countries) plus the 4 country pages that exist as hand-authored HTML outside that generator (`countries/egypt/`, `morocco/`, `sierra-leone/`, `liberia/` — these were never wired into `generate_country_pages.py`'s `COUNTRIES` dict in the first place, a separate pre-existing gap worth closing some day but out of scope here). Raised the slice count from 5→8 everywhere (`index.html` and the new country-page copies) per explicit user follow-up ("make this to 8 now on each country page"). Card selection is just `BOOKS[cty].slice(0,8)` — no new ranking logic, so it inherits the existing affiliate-first array order for free, consistent with the 2026-08-07 standing rule.
- **`countries/{egypt,morocco,sierra-leone,liberia}/index.html` are NOT generated by `generate_country_pages.py`**: confirmed by grepping the `COUNTRIES` dict for `'EG'`/`'MA'`/`'SL'`/`'LR'` (absent) and by running the generator (produces exactly 19 files, matching the dict). Any future template change to country pages (this one included) needs applying twice: once in the generator, once by hand to these 4 files — used a small Python script with `assert` guards (marker must appear exactly once, target string not already present) to apply the identical edit to all 4 safely rather than four manual Edit calls that could drift from each other.
- **`?v=` cache-bust discipline confirmed again**: bumped `shared.js?v=12→13` across all ~1,488 `.html` files (`grep -rl 'shared\.js?v=12' --include="*.html" . | xargs sed -i '' 's/shared\.js?v=12/shared.js?v=13/g'` — plain `-l`/no `-Z`, piped through `xargs` without `-0`, works fine on macOS BSD grep/xargs as long as no path contains a space/newline, unlike the `-Z`/`-0` combo documented as broken in the 2026-08-07 entry above) plus the 8 Python generators that hardcode the tag. Re-ran `scripts/validate_site.py`, `scripts/audit_titles.py`, and `scripts/compliance_check.py` after — all exit 0.
- **Git push race with the always-on automation fleet**: both this task's commits got `! [rejected] (fetch first)` on first push attempt because bot workflows (`leagues`, `Transfer news`, `live: hourly update`, `doctor: auto-heal`) committed to `main` in the few minutes this session was working. `git fetch` + inspect `git log --oneline HEAD..origin/main` + confirm no file-path overlap with what this session touched, then `git pull --rebase origin main` (clean, no conflicts both times since the bots only touch `data/*.json` and blog/transfer content, never `assets/shared.js` or generator `.py` files) resolved both. Never force-push here — this repo has a large always-running automation fleet, so `main` moves under you constantly and a plain `fetch` + `rebase` is the correct default response every time, not a sign something went wrong.

### Code Patterns (2026-08-08) — Added g4f as a free, keyless LLM tier between Groq and local Ollama; also: mid-session edits can get silently swept into an automated "deploy:" commit and partially lost

- **Follow-up to the Ollama 3b→8b fix earlier today**: user asked to add another free, no-signup, no-API-key LLM source rather than relying solely on the slow local model. Evaluated two options live before picking one: DuckDuckGo AI chat via the `ddgs` package (already a dependency, used elsewhere in this repo for free search) turned out to have had its `.chat()` method **removed** in the currently-installed version (`ddgs==9.14.4` — confirmed via `dir(DDGS())`, only `books/extract/images/news/text/threads/videos` remain, no chat). Pollinations.ai's free text API still works for bare anonymous requests but returns `402 Payment Required` the moment a `system` param or explicit `model` param is added (confirmed live via curl) — its own error message says the legacy anonymous tier is being deprecated. Neither was viable as a real `system_prompt` + `user_message` fact-checking/article-writing tier.
- **What worked**: `g4f` (`pip install g4f`, github.com/xtekky/gpt4free, ~60k-star open source project) — aggregates many free reverse-engineered LLM front-ends behind one OpenAI-style client interface, genuinely no signup/key/billing. Live-tested with real prompts before integrating: `gpt-4o-mini` answered a realistic fact-check prompt in 1.7s and a full 700-900 word article-generation prompt in 8.8s (722 words, correctly did NOT invent the exact transfer-fee fabrication from this morning's incident) — both dramatically faster and more instruction-compliant than local Ollama's multi-minute CPU inference.
- **Integration** (`agents/python/llm.py`): added as tier 5, between Groq (tiers 1-4) and local Ollama (now tier 6) in `_ask_with_fallback()` — tried first among the free/keyless options since it's hosted (seconds) vs. Ollama's CPU-bound minutes. Models tried in order: `gpt-4o-mini` → `gpt-4` → `llama-3.3-70b` (each a different underlying free provider). g4f's client has no reliable built-in timeout, so `_ask_g4f()` wraps the call in a `ThreadPoolExecutor` with a hard 60s wall-clock cap — a hung/dead g4f provider can't stall the pipeline. Mirrors the existing `_groq_exhausted_this_process` pattern with a new `_g4f_exhausted_this_process` flag so a single `generate_post()` call's two `ask()` invocations (draft + fact-check) don't both re-probe already-dead g4f models from scratch. Purely additive: package-missing is caught via `except ImportError` exactly like the existing Anthropic client guard, so an environment without `g4f` installed just silently skips this tier rather than breaking.
- **Real bug caught mid-task, worth remembering for any future editing session in this repo**: while making a sequence of edits to `llm.py`, one `Edit` call's changes appeared to "vanish" — a `NameError: _g4f_client is not defined` on a live test proved an entire function block I'd just added was missing from the file, even though later edits built on top of it were still present. Root cause was **not** a tool bug: this repo runs a **local** automated deploy pipeline (separate from the remote GitHub Actions bots) that periodically does the equivalent of `git add -A && commit -m "deploy: <timestamp> UTC" && push`, visible in `git log` as `deploy: 2026-08-07 23:35 UTC` etc. It fired mid-session, committed whatever partial state of my in-progress edit sequence was on disk at that instant, and something in its push-conflict recovery (same fetch/rebase-or-reset shape already documented in workflow YAMLs across this repo) discarded uncommitted changes made after its snapshot. **Fix/lesson**: when a multi-step edit to a file in this repo needs to survive intact, don't trust incremental `Edit` calls to persist across a long working session — write the complete final file content in one atomic `Write` call, verify immediately (`py_compile` + `grep` for the key additions), and `git add`+`commit`+`push` within the same turn, before doing anything else. This is really the same "commit immediately, don't leave work sitting uncommitted" standing rule already in `CLAUDE.md`, just discovered the hard way: uncommitted != safe here, even for a few minutes, because this repo's automation fleet (now confirmed to include at least one *local* process, not just remote CI) is actively touching the working tree on its own clock.
- **Not live-tested against the real 5-minute transfer-news CI loop** (no workflow triggered from this session) — verified via local `py_compile`, a real g4f call against the actual committed `llm.py` (simulating Groq-exhausted via `llm._groq_exhausted_this_process = True`, confirmed it routes to g4f and returns a correct, fast fact-check response), and reading the pushed `origin/main` copy back to confirm the g4f block survived the push. Worth checking the next live Groq-exhausted `transfer_news.yml` run for `[llm] Groq ... exhausted or empty — trying g4f` followed by a successful g4f response, and whether the fact-check pass rate visibly improves over the 0% seen this morning.

### Errors to Never Repeat (2026-08-08) — 4 more content workflows (`breaking_news.yml`, `content_backfill.yml`, `agent_translate.yml`, `agent5_plan.yml`) exposed to the same "job-timeout looks like `cancelled`, not `failure`, so no safety net retries it" gap

- **Found via a live example**: `breaking_news.yml`'s 13:49 UTC run on 2026-08-08 hit its own 40-min `timeout-minutes` mid-generation and GitHub reported the run as `conclusion: cancelled`. Neither `workflow_watchdog.yml` (fires on `workflow_run` `conclusion == 'failure'`) nor `retry_failed.yml`'s `check_and_retry_failures()` (also checks `conclusion == "failure"` only) retries a `cancelled` run — and `retry_failed.yml`'s separate `check_missed_schedules()` only fires when NO run started for a slot at all, so a run that started on time and then got timeout-killed mid-way reads as "up to date for its slot" and is never retriggered either. The result: a stuck/slow run silently drops a whole 3x-daily content slot with zero automated recovery, until the next natural schedule tick.
- **Root cause of the slowness itself, confirmed in the run's own log**: `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` isn't configured (same gap as the 2026-08-07 performance_check.py finding above), Groq's free tier was exhausted across all 4 models, g4f's free providers were also exhausted/broken (`Pollinations: ResponseError: Error 404`), so the chain fell all the way to local Ollama — installing + pulling a fresh 4.9GB `llama3.1:8b` model (upgraded from a smaller 3b model the *same day*, per `llm.py`'s own dated comment, which silently invalidated the 40-min job-timeout sizing that had been calibrated against the smaller/faster model). `llm.py`'s own timeouts allow up to ~38 min for a single Ollama round-trip (180s install + 900s pull + 1200s generate) — comfortably capable of exhausting a 40-min job on its own, before counting the 2-3 sequential LLM calls these workflows each make per run.
- **Fix applied — bounded per-call `timeout` wrappers, not a bigger job ceiling** (raising the job timeout was already tried once for this exact symptom on 2026-08-02 and proved insufficient again the same day the Ollama model got bigger): added shell-level `timeout N python ...` around every LLM-calling invocation in `breaking_news.yml` (600s x3 calls), `content_backfill.yml` (1800s), `agent_translate.yml` (1800s), and `agent5_plan.yml` (480s, tight against its 10-min job ceiling but that workflow is weekly/low-frequency/best-effort by design). A `timeout`-killed call now fails with a normal non-zero exit well inside the job's own ceiling, instead of the whole job being externally killed by GitHub — turning an invisible `cancelled` into a normal, retryable `failure` (or, for the `set +e` / `exit_code` pattern used by 3 of these 4 workflows, letting every step *after* the capped call — regenerate pages, commit, push, deploy-trigger, report — still run with whatever was completed before the cap, rather than being skipped entirely by an external kill mid-step). Also bumped `breaking_news.yml`'s job timeout 40→45 min as a last-resort margin, now sized against the per-call caps as a backstop rather than as the primary bound.
- **Did not touch `llm.py`'s own Ollama timeouts** (900s pull / 1200s generate) — tightening those without live measurement of actual GH Actions runner throughput risked breaking genuinely-slow-but-working generations instead of just failing fast on genuinely-stuck ones. The per-workflow shell `timeout` wrapper achieves the same "don't let this consume the whole job" goal without needing to guess at Ollama's real-world timing.
- **Found, while investigating, that a concurrent session was independently fixing the *same class* of bug in `transfer_news.yml`/`llm.py` at the same time** (a `CONSECUTIVE_FLAGGED`/`FLAGGED_THRESHOLD` tripwire so a fact-checker-rejecting-everything loop fails loud instead of running green while publishing nothing for 12+ hours, plus a new g4f fallback tier) — left entirely untouched/unstaged, not mine to commit. Also found and removed a stray `AGENT-KNOWLEDGE 2.md` duplicate (a filesystem artifact from the same concurrent access, fully superseded by the real tracked file, zero unique content) before committing this entry, and re-read the file's live tail immediately before appending to avoid clobbering the concurrent session's own already-pushed entries.
- **Lesson, generalising past the specific workflows fixed today**: any workflow with `cancel-in-progress: false` + a fixed `timeout-minutes` + one or more unbounded LLM/subprocess calls has this exact blind spot, regardless of whether it's ever actually been observed to time out yet — `conclusion: cancelled` from a job-timeout is indistinguishable, to every retry mechanism in this repo, from a run nobody ever started. The fix is always the same shape: bound the risky call(s) with a shell `timeout` comfortably inside the job's own ceiling, so the job can finish (and thus report a real, retryable status) even in the worst case, rather than trusting the job-level timeout to never be reached.

### Errors to Never Repeat (2026-08-08) — g4f's own auto-provider-selection was a total no-op in production; fixed by pinning explicit providers + added a self-healing tripwire to transfer_news.yml

- **The g4f tier added earlier today (previous entry above) did not actually work in CI** despite passing every local test. Live run `31271953519` (18:27 UTC) showed the real failure: `[llm] g4f gpt-4o-mini failed: RetryProvider failed: GithubCopilot: MissingAuthError: GitHub Copilot OAuth not configured. Please run 'g4f auth github-copilot' to authenticate.` and, on a later attempt in the same run, `Nvidia: PaymentRequiredError: Error 402: No cake credits.` — g4f's default `Client()` auto-selects from its full ~90-provider pool per model, and on the actual GitHub Actions runner (different from this dev machine — likely IP/geo-dependent provider routing) it kept landing on providers that need real credentials. The tier fell through to Ollama every time, exactly as if it didn't exist, and Ollama (even at 8b) still occasionally hallucinated and got flagged.
- **Fix**: pinned `_G4F_PROVIDERS` in `agents/python/llm.py` to 3 specific provider classes instead of leaving provider selection to g4f. Found them by testing ~10 candidates live (`python3 -c "import g4f.Provider as P; ... needs_auth is False and working is not False"` to shortlist, then real API calls against each with both a short fact-check-style JSON prompt and a full 700+ word article prompt): `WeWordle` (gpt-4o-mini, ~2-11s, handled both prompt shapes correctly), `OperaAria` (aria, ~30s, the only other one reliable on the long article prompt), `Cloudflare` (llama-3.3-70b, fast on short prompts but throws `UnboundLocalError` on longer ones — kept anyway since a failure there is harmless, just falls through). Ruled out along the way: `Pollinations` (`402 Payment Required` the moment a `system` param is added — same "anonymous-only, deprecated for anything else" wall found earlier today), `PhindAi`/`TeachAnything` (403 security checks), `Qwen` (500 errors), `Pi`/`LMArena` (need a local browser automation driver — `zendriver`/`nodriver` — not installed), `HuggingSpace`/`GLM`/`DeepInfra` (broken or hung indefinitely), `Yqcloud` (works but replies in Chinese regardless of the system prompt — useless for this site's English content, would just fail JSON parsing downstream every time). **Re-run this same test sweep if g4f is ever touched again** — individual free front-ends break/get patched without notice, this is not a "set once" list.
- **Also added a real self-healing mechanism** (`transfer_news.yml`), since the user explicitly asked for automatic detection+recovery, not just a one-off patch: the loop now tracks `CONSECUTIVE_FLAGGED`, incremented whenever `agent_transfer_post.py`'s output contains "Fact-checker held back this article" (captured via `... | tee /tmp/transfer_cycle_output.log` + grep, `${PIPESTATUS[0]}` used for the real exit code since the pipe would otherwise mask it), reset to 0 on any cycle that isn't a fact-check rejection (crucially, *not* incremented on ordinary "no fresh news right now" cycles — those are a normal, expected outcome, not a failure). At 5 consecutive rejections (~25 minutes), the loop deliberately sets `EXIT_CODE=1` and breaks, so the job reports FAILED instead of silently sleeping forever. This is the actual fix for the invisibility problem from the 2026-08-07/08 incident: `workflow_watchdog.yml`/`retry_failed.yml` already exist and already retry failed workflows automatically, but they had literally nothing to react to before this, because "fact-checker rejected every draft" was indistinguishable from "a quiet news day" at the exit-code level. A fresh retried run gets a fresh process, which re-probes Groq/g4f from scratch (the `_groq_exhausted_this_process`/`_g4f_exhausted_this_process` flags in `llm.py` are process-scoped and only ever latch on, never clear, within one run) — so this tripwire is what actually lets the existing retry infrastructure do its job here, not a new retry mechanism of its own.
- **Confirmed live (again) that this repo runs at least one *local* automated "deploy" process**, separate from remote GitHub Actions bots: mid-session, both `requirements.txt` and `transfer_news.yml`'s edits showed up already committed (as generic `deploy: <timestamp> UTC` commits) and already pushed to `origin/main` before this session ran its own `git add`/`commit`/`push` — confirmed by `git log --oneline -1` matching `git log --oneline origin/main -1` exactly after a fresh `.py`/`.yml` edit that this session had not yet committed. Also found a stray untracked `AGENT-KNOWLEDGE 2.md` (437KB) and unrelated modifications to 4 other workflow YAMLs mid-session, none of which this session made — stashed safely (`git stash push -u`, never discarded) rather than committed or deleted, consistent with the git-safety rule to treat unfamiliar state as someone else's in-progress work until proven otherwise. **Practical implication for any future session**: don't trust that an uncommitted edit will still be there a few minutes later here — commit immediately after verifying, per the existing "Always Commit and Push" standing rule, and if a diff unexpectedly comes back empty right after an edit, check `git log` before assuming something went wrong — it may just mean the deploy bot already committed it for you.
- **Not yet confirmed against a live Groq-exhausted CI run with the pinned providers** — verified via direct local calls to `WeWordle`/`OperaAria`/`Cloudflare` through the actual committed `llm.py` (simulating Groq exhaustion via `llm._groq_exhausted_this_process = True`), and via `bash -n`/PyYAML validation of the workflow script, but not by watching a real `transfer_news.yml` run hit the g4f tier live. Next thing worth checking: does a live run show `[llm] Groq ... exhausted or empty — trying g4f` followed by a successful `WeWordle`/`OperaAria` response (not another `RetryProvider failed`), and does the `CONSECUTIVE_FLAGGED` counter ever actually reach 5 in practice now that a working free tier sits ahead of Ollama.

### Code Patterns (2026-08-08) — New monetized feature: paid "Country Sponsor Placements" strip on all 23 country pages, built entirely by reusing existing infra

- **User asked for every `countries/<slug>/index.html` page to show a top-of-page row of big bookmaker cards (reference: a freebets.com screenshot — logo tile, offer headline, big CTA button, T&Cs fine print), monetized as brands paying a flat monthly fee for placement, restricted to brands with real affiliate links.** Before writing any code, investigated whether this needed new infrastructure — it didn't. `data/featured_listings.json`'s `_schema` already had `country_sponsor:<country>` listed as a valid slot prefix (unused, `listings: []` was empty) from an earlier design pass, and every `BOOKS[cty]` entry in `assets/shared.js` already carries a `terms` field with real per-brand fine print (e.g. "Code 1BONUSNG. 300% on first deposit up to ₦1,200,000. Wager x5 accumulators at min odds 1.40. 30 days. T&Cs. 18+.") — exactly the small print the screenshot shows under each card. Zero new data modeling was needed; only new rendering logic.
- **Implementation**: extended the slot format to `country_sponsor:<country-slug>:<position 1-5>` (position suffix added since the original schema only anticipated one sponsor per country, but the screenshot needs 5 ranked slots). `assets/shared.js`'s new `renderSponsorStrip()` is entirely self-contained — triggered via its own `document.addEventListener('DOMContentLoaded', ...)`, exactly like the existing `renderBrandsBar()` pattern — so wiring it into 23 pages needed **only** inserting an empty `<div id="sponsor-strip" class="sponsor-strip"></div>` placeholder into each page's HTML (plus `generate_country_pages.py`'s template, for future regenerations). No per-page inline-script edits were needed, which is what made this tractable at 23-page scale — CLAUDE.md's existing "Featured Listings rendering scope note" had already flagged that country pages don't share rendering logic through `shared.js` and warned that wiring a new placement there "needs... first centralizing... into shared.js — treat that as its own prerequisite task." Making the new function self-triggering and self-fetching sidestepped that prerequisite entirely rather than doing it.
- **Brand-slug matching without adding a new data field**: rather than adding a `slug` key to every `BOOKS[cty]` entry (huge mechanical diff across ~23 countries × brands), derived it from the existing `BRAND_DOMAINS[abbr]` map (ABBR → real domain, e.g. `'1X':'1xbet.com'`) by taking the domain's first label (`'1xbet.com'.split('.')[0]` → `'1xbet'`). This matches `agents/python/utils/affiliate_links.py`'s `BRAND_SLUGS` values by construction for every existing brand, so `featured_listings.json`'s `brand_slug` field needs no new naming convention.
- **Eligibility enforcement lives entirely client-side, not in the Python CLI**: `assets/shared.js` already had `AFFILIATE_DOMAINS`/`isAffiliate()`/`affiliateBooks()` (used by the "Brands With a Real Affiliate Link Are Always Top-of-List" rule) — reused directly rather than writing a second gate. `agent_brand_partnerships.py --add-listing` only validates slot *format* (`country_sponsor:<slug>:<1-5>` via regex) and prints a reminder about the affiliate-link requirement; it deliberately does NOT hard-block selling a slot to a non-affiliate brand, because Python has no reliable access to the live tracking URLs in `shared.js` without parsing JS (would violate the "no fourth hardcoded brand schema" lesson already documented in this file's Phase-2 roadmap notes). The actual enforcement is in `renderSponsorStrip()`: a paid entry that doesn't resolve to an affiliate-linked `BOOKS` entry is silently skipped and the slot falls back to an unlabeled organic pick — same "expired listing silently stops showing" philosophy already used by `bookmaker_page_template.py`'s badge.
- **Found and fixed 3 pre-existing bugs while building this, all directly adjacent to the code being touched**: (1) `AFFILIATE_DOMAINS` in `shared.js` was missing 3 of the 9 documented tracking domains from the 2026-08-07 standing rule (`goaffnk.com`, `track.trkbxa.click`, `track.bettapartners.co.za`) — silently under-counting which brands qualify as "affiliate-linked" everywhere that array is used, not just in this new feature. (2) The existing "inject About link into nav on every page" snippet in `shared.js` computed its relative-root path with `depth>1?'../'.repeat(depth-1):(depth===1?'../':'')`, which is off by one for 2-level-deep pages: on `/countries/nigeria/` it produced `../about/` (resolves to the non-existent `/countries/about/`) instead of the correct `../../about/`. Caught this only because a near-identical formula was copied for the new feature's own fetch-path helper and a live Playwright test showed the JSON fetch itself 404ing at the wrong path — extracted the fix into one shared `_pathRoot()` helper (`location.pathname.split('/').filter(Boolean)`, `'../'.repeat(segs.length)`) and pointed both call sites at it, rather than leaving two independently-wrong copies. (3) `generate_country_pages.py --force`-style regeneration is NOT safe to run blindly: doing so on all 23 pages during this session reverted a prior `seo_check.py --fix` meta-description truncation on 9 of them (the live pages had been shortened to fit the ≤155-char limit; the generator's own template still had the pre-truncation text) — reverted immediately via `git checkout`, then made all further edits by scripted string-replacement against the *live* files instead of full regeneration. **The generator's static template content has drifted from several auto-healed live pages and is not a safe source of truth to regenerate from wholesale** — this is a latent landmine for any future session that reaches for `python3 generate_country_pages.py` expecting a no-op.
- **Visual/layout gotcha**: `grid-template-columns:repeat(auto-fill,minmax(210px,1fr))` looks great when there are 4-5+ cards (stretches edge-to-edge) but looks broken for thin markets — Ethiopia and DR Congo only have 1-2 real-affiliate-linked `BOOKS` entries, and `auto-fill` still computes phantom empty tracks across the full container width, then `1fr` stretches/distributes into them, producing a giant near-empty green strip with one tiny card. Fixed by switching to `auto-fit` (collapses unfilled tracks instead of leaving them phantom) with a **capped fixed max** (`minmax(210px,280px)`, not `1fr`) so leftover space is never force-distributed onto the few real cards either. Verified both the 4-card (Nigeria) and 1-card (DR Congo) cases visually via a local `python3 -m http.server` + Playwright screenshot before considering this done — this class of "looks fine with lots of data, breaks with little" grid bug won't show up in `validate_site.py`/`compliance_check.py` since those don't render CSS.
- **Mid-task scope trim from the user**: after seeing the rendered screenshot, user said "only show 6 and not 8" — referring to the pre-existing, unrelated `renderFeatCards()` "⭐ Top Picks" grid (`.slice(0,8)`) visible lower on the same page in the screenshot, not the new 5-slot sponsor strip. Confirmed by grepping for the literal `.slice(0,8)` string before editing, since `index.html` and `casino/index.html` also contain that exact string for their own unrelated card grids — scoped the fix to just `countries/*/index.html` + `generate_country_pages.py` (24 files) rather than touching those other pages, since the whole task was scoped to country pages only.
- **Testing approach for a static site with no dev server**: `python3 -m http.server <port>` from the repo root plus Playwright (already installed, `pip show playwright` confirmed 1.60.0) was enough to validate a client-side-fetch feature end-to-end — including temporarily injecting a fake entry into `data/featured_listings.json` to prove the "Sponsored" badge path works, then restoring the original empty file via a pre-edit backup copy before committing. `wait_until="networkidle"` hung indefinitely on this site (GA4 `google-analytics.com/g/collect` beacon never quiesces) — use `wait_until="domcontentloaded"` plus an explicit `wait_for_timeout()` instead for any future Playwright check against a SifuFinds page.

### Errors to Never Repeat (2026-08-08) — g4f's 3-provider pin in `llm.py` was all-or-nothing: one renamed provider silently killed all three

- **What happened**: a same-day earlier fix (`6fd4d33fa`, concurrent session) pinned g4f's free fallback tier to 3 specific no-auth provider classes (`WeWordle`, `OperaAria`, `Cloudflare`) after confirming g4f's own auto-selection routed to auth-gated providers in production. All three imports lived under one `try: ... except ImportError`. Confirmed live on a normal `transfer_news.yml` scheduled cycle the same evening: `cannot import name 'WeWordle' from 'g4f.Provider'` — g4f (pinned loosely, `g4f>=7.9.0`, no upper bound in `requirements.txt`) had drifted to a version on the GitHub Actions runner where `WeWordle` had been renamed/removed, and the single shared `except ImportError` took `OperaAria` and `Cloudflare` down with it even though neither was actually broken. The entire g4f tier silently went from 3 working providers to 0, forcing every call straight to the slow local-Ollama fallback that day's other fixes (bounded `timeout` wrappers, above) exist specifically to survive, not eliminate the need for.
- **Confirmed NOT a local-environment artifact**: this repo's own local Python environment has all three provider classes present and importable right now — the drift is specifically GH Actions-runner-vs-local, consistent with a fresh `pip install` picking up whatever g4f version is current at run time, not whatever was true when the pin was tested.
- **Fix**: split the single 3-import `try/except ImportError` into (1) one `try/except` around only `from g4f.client import Client` (the real "is g4f installed at all" check) and (2) a loop that does `getattr(g4f.Provider, provider_name)` for each of the 3 pinned names individually inside its own `try/except AttributeError`, appending only the ones that resolve to `_G4F_PROVIDERS`. A provider that's gone this week degrades the tier to 2 (or 1, or 0) working providers instead of 0 outright. Verified: (a) normal case still resolves all 3 providers via a real module import, (b) simulated the exact failure (a provider name that doesn't exist) and confirmed the other 2 survive and get logged individually rather than one opaque tier-wide skip message.
- **Deliberately did not exact-pin the g4f version** in `requirements.txt` — the module's own comment already documents that "individual providers break/get patched without notice" as an expected, ongoing maintenance reality for this dependency; per-provider resilience in the code is the fix that survives future churn, a version pin would only survive until the next time someone bumps it for an unrelated reason.
- **Lesson**: when pinning multiple related-but-independent things (provider classes, model names, feed URLs, brand slugs) behind a single import/init block, one broken item taking down all the others is a shape of bug that's easy to introduce without noticing, especially when the block "worked in every local test" — the failure only shows up when the *environment* drifts (a fresh runner installs a newer dependency version), not when the *code* changes. Prefer building the pinned list incrementally with a per-item try/except over a single all-or-nothing block whenever the items don't actually depend on each other.

### Errors to Never Repeat (2026-08-08) — 6 concurrent Claude Code sessions were sharing one working directory; fixed with per-session git worktrees

- **Root cause of the whole "files mysteriously revert mid-edit" mystery from earlier today** (the g4f code block that vanished from `llm.py`, the stray untracked `AGENT-KNOWLEDGE 2.md`, `requirements.txt` edits appearing already-committed before this session committed them): `ListAgents` showed **5 other interactive Claude Code sessions** running at the same time as this one, and `git worktree list` confirmed all 6 were checked out against the exact same single directory (`~/Desktop/Sifu Finds`) and the same `main` branch. Any session's file Write/Edit, or any session's `git reset --hard`/`git rebase`/`git stash`, was operating on literally the same files every other session had open — a full-file `Write` from one session mid-edit could (and did) silently discard another session's in-progress uncommitted changes, since there is no locking between concurrent agent processes editing the same path.
- **Fix, confirmed with the user rather than applied unilaterally** (this affects 5 other active sessions' environments, not just this one): set up `git worktree`-based isolation. Each session gets its own directory + its own local branch, all sharing the same underlying `.git` object store and the same GitHub remote, so file edits from one session can never physically touch another session's checkout again. Full setup instructions and the exact publish-to-main command pattern (a worktree branch can never be literally named `main`, since git refuses to check out the same branch in two worktrees — publish with `git fetch origin main && git rebase origin/main && git push origin HEAD:main` instead of the usual `git push`) are documented in `~/Desktop/sifu-finds-worktrees/README.md` (deliberately kept *outside* the repo itself, not git-tracked, since it's operational documentation about how sessions are organized, not project content).
- **This session's own worktree**: `~/Desktop/sifu-finds-worktrees/claude-sonnet-main` on branch `worktree/claude-sonnet-main`, with its own dedicated `.venv` (deliberately not shared/symlinked from the main repo's `.venv` — sharing one Python environment across concurrent sessions reintroduces the same category of race this whole fix exists to eliminate, e.g. one session's `pip install` running mid-import in another). This very entry was written, committed, and pushed from that worktree as the first real end-to-end test of the new setup.
- **User explicitly chose NOT to have this session message/move the other 5 sessions** — they said they'd move sessions over themselves. Don't proactively interrupt sibling sessions with unsolicited coordination messages; the shared directory (`~/Desktop/Sifu Finds`) remains fully functional for any session still working there, this was additive, not a forced migration.
- **Not yet exercised under a real conflict**: this setup was validated for a clean, no-conflict push (`git push --dry-run` succeeded, then a real push landed cleanly). It has not yet been tested against an actual overlapping edit between two worktree branches rebasing onto `main` around the same time — should still work via the normal `git rebase` conflict-resolution flow (resolve conflict markers, `git add`, `git rebase --continue`), just not empirically confirmed in this repo yet.
- **Confirmed the resolution flow works, from the old shared directory, shortly after**: a session still on `~/Desktop/Sifu Finds` (not yet migrated) hit this exact scenario twice while landing an unrelated `llm.py` fix — a content conflict in this file against the entry above. `git rebase --continue` repeatedly refused ("You must edit all merge conflicts...") even with `git status`/`git ls-files -u` both confirming zero unmerged paths, consistent with another concurrent process touching `.git/` internals at the same moment — exactly the risk this fix exists to eliminate. Recovered by aborting the stuck rebase (the fix content was independently safe either way, having already been swept into a commit by this repo's local auto-deploy pipeline) and switching to a plain `git merge --no-edit origin/main` instead of rebase, which hit the same content conflicts (this file, plus 4 bot-generated state/sitemap files this session never touched, resolved by taking origin's version) but resolved and committed cleanly on the first attempt. **Lesson: in this repo's specific shared/multi-session environment, prefer `git fetch && git merge --no-edit origin/main` over `git rebase origin/main` for routine sync-with-origin — a single merge commit is far less exposed to concurrent `.git/` interference than a multi-step rebase replaying commits one at a time.**
- **Hit the identical stuck-rebase symptom independently, same day, still on the shared directory**: another interactive rebase (not started by this session — 1 commit already picked, 1 remaining, "all conflicts fixed: run git rebase --continue") sat idle for 5+ minutes with zero unmerged paths, and `git rebase --continue` repeatedly failed with the same misleading "You must edit all merge conflicts" message. Asked the user for explicit confirmation before running `git rebase --abort` on a rebase this session didn't start (destructive, and the work might have belonged to another live session) — they confirmed. Root-caused the actual damage afterward: the abort discarded this session's own **uncommitted working-tree edits** to `agent3_social.py`, `agent_sports_blog.py`, and `CLAUDE.md` (files the concurrent rebase's commits happened to also touch), while edits to files the rebase never touched (`agent_trending_keywords.py`, `news_fetcher.py`, the new `agent_country_trending_writer.py`) had already been swept into a `deploy:` bot commit moments earlier and survived intact. **Lesson: after any `git rebase --abort` recovery in this shared environment, immediately grep every file touched this session for the specific markers/symbols just added — don't assume "abort succeeded cleanly" means "my in-progress edits survived." A partial loss (some files reverted, others fine) is the most likely outcome, not all-or-nothing, and is easy to miss without an explicit per-file check.**

### Errors to Never Repeat (2026-08-08) — Hostinger deploy pipeline: three independent bugs compounded into ~19 hours of new posts 404ing live

- **User report**: "blog post are not working". Found two genuine new posts (`esports-betting-africa-2026`, `springboks-six-nations-betting-guide`) fully built, validated, and committed, but 404ing live. Root cause was not one bug but three, found in sequence as each fix exposed the next:
  1. **Cooldown-reset-off-skip-runs bug** in `deploy_hostinger.yml`'s `check-cooldown` job: it measured elapsed time since the workflow's *last run*, not since the last *real upload attempt*. On this repo's push cadence (bot commits every few minutes), a chain of skip-only runs kept re-arming each other's 15-min cooldown window, so a real deploy attempt could be starved for 40+ minutes even after Hostinger's rate limit had likely cleared. Fixed by keying the cooldown check specifically on the "Deploy to Hostinger via MCP (with retry)" *step's* own conclusion (not the job's overall conclusion, and not just "did the workflow run") — a run that fails at an earlier validation step without ever touching Hostinger's API must never count toward the 429 backoff (see bug 3 below for why this distinction mattered in practice).
